3:["$","$L54",null,{"as":"h2","id":"the-problem-with-knowing-where-you-are","children":"The problem with knowing where you are"}]
4:["$","p",null,{"children":["Put a robot in a corridor with three identical doors and ask it where it is. It has a door\ndetector, and the detector says: ",["$","em",null,{"children":"door"}],"."]}]
5:["$","p",null,{"children":["Now what? A robot that insists on a single answer must pick one of the three doors, and it will\nbe wrong two times out of three. Worse, it will be wrong ",["$","em",null,{"children":"confidently"}],", and every computation\ndownstream — the path it plans, the obstacle it swerves around — inherits that false certainty\nwithout any record that a guess was ever made."]}]
6:["$","p",null,{"children":["The probabilistic answer is to refuse the question as posed. The robot does not know where it\nis; it knows something weaker and far more useful, which is how plausible each location is given\neverything that has happened. That object is a ",["$","strong",null,{"children":"belief"}],", and this chapter is about how to keep\none up to date."]}]

b:["$","p",null,{"children":[["$","strong",null,{"children":"Sensing multiplies; moving convolves."}]," The green dashed curve is the measurement likelihood.\nCorrection multiplies the belief by it pointwise, which is why peaks get sharper and mass\nconcentrates.
